❯ Let's discuss about Playwright first.                                         

  Searched for 1 pattern, read 2 files, listed 1 directory (ctrl+o to expand)
● Current Playwright transcript-extraction setup (domains/ycs/transcript/):

  - Headed CDP only (playwright.playwright.svc.cluster.local:9222) — headless is
    blocked by YouTube for captions, config kept but unused for this path.
  - Concurrency: 5 pooled browser contexts (MAX_CONCURRENT=5,
    CONTEXT_POOL_SIZE=5), capped there because 5 concurrent pages OOM the headed
    pod below 4Gi.
  - PoT provider sidecar (bgutil-pot on :4416) mints a token every scrape —
    YouTube's timedtext endpoint silently 200s-with-empty-body without it.
  - Resource blocking: aggressive BLOCK_PATTERNS/BLOCK_RESOURCE_TYPES strip
    video/ad/analytics/image/font traffic to cut render contention that was
    causing "panel not loaded" failures.
  - Retry taxonomy: PERMANENT_ERRORS (no transcript/unavailable/private) vs
    RETRYABLE_ERRORS (button/panel/timeout/navigation/context), MAX_RETRIES=2,
    connect retry up to 6 attempts (5s→~60s backoff).
  - Batch driver: fetch_transcriptions_batch() is ES-cache-aware, checkpoints
    every 10 videos (DEFAULT_CHUNK_SIZE).
  - service.py is 1673 LOC — the actual scrape/DOM logic lives there.

  What's the angle you want to dig into — captcha/blocking risk, throughput,
  reliability of the retry logic, or something else in service.py?

● Recommendation

  Stay on Chromium via CDP, but swap the playwright package for patchright
  (Kaliiiiiiiiii-Vinyzu/patchright-python) — a byte-for-byte drop-in that keeps
  your exact remote-CDP-pod architecture (connect_over_cdp() unchanged) while
  patching the specific CDP leaks (Runtime.enable, Console.enable) that anti-bot
  layers fingerprint. It's the only option that fixes the CAPTCHA pain point
  with zero architecture change. RAM and speed are separate axes that no drop-in
  engine swap solves without a bigger rewrite — those stay in the "script
  improvements" bucket.

  Obscura is not a fit: it's a from-scratch Rust rendering engine (not
  Chromium), so it carries real compatibility risk against YouTube's
  bleeding-edge Polymer/web-components SPA — no evidence anyone's run it against
  YouTube specifically, and a broken transcript-panel render is worse than a
  slow one.

  How to apply it

  1. uv remove playwright && uv add patchright (or add alongside, don't run
     both).
  2. In apps/fastapi/domains/ycs/transcript/service.py, change the import (from 
     playwright.async_api import ... → from patchright.async_api import ...) and
     nothing else — connect_over_cdp(CDP_HEADED) is identical.
  3. The remote pod itself (playwright.playwright.svc.cluster.local:9222) stays
     a stock Chromium/CDP server — Patchright patches the client library, not
     the browser binary, so no Helm/pod changes needed.
  4. Re-run a batch and watch for captcha-trigger rate over the next several
     ingestion runs — this is the only way to know it actually moved the needle
     for YouTube specifically (no public benchmark tests YouTube).

  Comparison table

  Approach: Patchright (Chromium, patched Playwright)
  Maturity: Active, 3.2k★, maintained by original author
  Latest (date): v1.62.3 (Jun 2026), last commit Aug 18 2026
  Remote-CDP-pod compatible?: ✅ yes — connect_over_cdp() unchanged
  Key trade-off: Same RAM/speed as vanilla Playwright (still real headed
  Chromium)
  When to pick: Default — lowest-risk captcha mitigation
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Approach: nodriver / zendriver (CDP-direct, no WebDriver)
  Maturity: zendriver active (cdpdriver org), issues as recent as Apr 2026
  Latest (date): zendriver rolling releases
  Remote-CDP-pod compatible?: ❌ no — remote-connect-to-existing-browser is an
  open, unresolved feature request
  Key trade-off: ~80–120MB/instance vs Playwright's much heavier footprint; best

  detection score (0/28 blocked in benchmark) — but forces collapsing your
  separate browser-pod into the worker process
  When to pick: If RAM becomes the harder constraint than captcha, and you're
  willing to re-architect away from the pod-separation pattern
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Approach: Camoufox (patched Firefox)
  Maturity: Active, has own Docker/remote-WS-server pattern
  Latest (date): Firefox 135.0.1-beta.24 base (per May 2026 benchmark)
  Remote-CDP-pod compatible?: ✅ yes — exposes a Playwright-compatible WebSocket

  server, so the "separate remote pod" pattern survives
  Key trade-off: 0% headless-detection in benchmark (best stealth), but "among
  the slowest," and every DOM selector in service.py needs re-validation
  against Firefox rendering
  When to pick: If Patchright alone doesn't move the captcha rate and you're OK
  re-testing the whole transcript-panel scrape path
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Approach: Obscura (Rust, non-Chromium engine)
  Maturity: Active, 26.9k★, has CDP-serve + MCP mode
  Latest (date): 1,128 commits, stable releases
  Remote-CDP-pod compatible?: ✅ architecturally yes
  Key trade-off: No Chromium lineage → unproven against YouTube's SPA; a wrong
  bet risks new, harder-to-debug scrape failures
  When to pick: Not now — revisit if it publishes real-world SPA/YouTube
  compatibility data

  Evidence

  - Patchright is a drop-in connect_over_cdp replacement, actively maintained
    (last commit Aug 18 2026, v1.62.3): patchright-python, PyPI
  - Benchmark: nodriver 28/28 OK (0 blocked), Camoufox/Patchright 25/28,
    tools/versions/dates: Anti-Detect Browser Benchmark 2026 (May 13, 2026,
    updated Aug 15 2026) — YouTube was not a tested target,
    single-IP/single-night, explicitly flagged as non-generalizable.
  - nodriver/zendriver remote-CDP-connect-to-existing-browser is an open,
    unanswered feature request, not shipped: zendriver issue #98
  - nodriver memory ~80–120MB/instance vs Playwright's heavier footprint:
    CapSolver comparison
  - Camoufox ships a Docker/remote-WebSocket-server pattern (pod-separation
    compatible), Firefox-level patches, "among the slowest": camoufox-docker,
    benchmark above.
  - Obscura: Rust engine + embedded V8, no Chromium dependency, CDP-serve + MCP,
    26.9k★, no YouTube/CAPTCHA-specific documentation: direct fetch of
    h4ckf0r0day/obscura.
  - CDP Runtime.enable leak — the classic Playwright/Puppeteer detection vector
    Patchright specifically patches — quietly stopped firing on stock Chrome
    sometime in 2025, but detectors are moving to multi-layered fingerprinting,
    not abandoning CDP checks: Castle.io, Rebrowser.

  Caveats & unknowns

  - No source tests YouTube specifically for any of these tools — every
    detection-rate number above is against generic
    Cloudflare/DataDome/PerimeterX-protected sites. YouTube's bot detection is
    Google's own stack; the extrapolation from these benchmarks is reasonable
    but unverified.
  - The benchmark's 13GB peak-RAM figure for Patchright looks like an
    outlier/measurement artifact (many-page crawl session, not per-instance) —
    don't take it as "Patchright is uniquely heavy"; it runs the same Chromium
    as vanilla Playwright.
  - Single-IP, single-night benchmark methodology is explicitly flagged by its
    own author as not production-predictive — treat rankings as directional, not
    authoritative.

  Bottom line for your three complaints: captcha → try Patchright first (cheap,
  real, zero-risk); RAM and speed → not solved by any drop-in swap, that's
  genuinely the "script improvements" track (context/concurrency tuning,
  resource blocking already partially done, session/cookie persistence, etc.) —
  want to move to that next?
✻ Baked for 2m 41s

❯ from all these options, is Obscura the fastest and best browser to connect to 
  Playwright scripts?                                                           

● Web Search("Obscura browser h4ckf0r0day benchmark speed RAM memory vs Chromium
            Playwright")
  ⎿  Did 1 search in 6s

● Fetch(https://github.com/h4ckf0r0day/obscura)
  ⎿  Received 487.7KB (200 OK)

● Fetch(https://wavect.io/blog/obscura-rust-browser-ai-agents/)
  ⎿  Received 37.8KB (200 OK)

● No — it's the fastest on paper, but not the best pick for this target.

  Speed/RAM claims: Obscura's own numbers (30MB RAM, 85ms page load vs Chrome's
  200MB+/500ms) are self-reported by the project, not independently verified,
  and predate its native-rendering-engine release (July fixtures, August
  renderer shipped after) — an independent reviewer flags them as inapplicable
  to current render-enabled workloads.

  The real problem for your case: Obscura is a from-scratch Rust rendering
  engine, not Chromium — it explicitly admits gaps in "long-tail CSS, some Web
  APIs, media playback, compositor effects" vs Chromium, and there's zero
  documentation or evidence of Shadow DOM / custom-elements support. YouTube's
  entire UI — including the transcript panel itself (ytd-transcript-renderer,
  ytd-transcript-segment-renderer) — is built on Polymer web-components sitting
  inside Shadow DOM. If Obscura's engine doesn't fully replicate that, your DOM
  selectors just silently stop finding the panel — a worse failure mode than
  slow/heavy Chromium.

  The independent review's own verdict: don't swap it into production — run a
  30-day pilot with a Chrome/Patchright fallback lane, benchmark against your
  actual corpus, not the vendor's numbers.

  Bottom line: Patchright stays the safe move for your captcha problem today
  (real Chromium, zero DOM risk). Obscura is worth a cheap isolated experiment —
  point it at one YouTube video's transcript panel and see if it
  renders/interacts correctly at all — before ever considering it for the real
  pipeline.

❯ but the claims about cloudflare being inspired by Obscura are true or false?  

● Web Search("Obscura browser Cloudflare inspired h4ckf0r0day")
  ⎿  Did 1 search in 3s

● Web Search(""Obscura" browser Cloudflare "inspired by" OR "based on" claim")
  ⎿  Did 1 search in 5s

● Web Search("Cloudflare Kitesurf browser blog announcement Obscura Blitz Stylo
            Rust WebAssembly")
  ⎿  Did 1 search in 6s

● Fetch(https://blog.cloudflare.com/kitesurf/)
  ⎿  Received 36.7KB (200 OK)

● True, but overstated as usually phrased. Cloudflare's own blog confirms it
  directly: "We got the initial inspiration from obscura, a headless engine 
  written in Rust for AI automation that has 'no Chrome, no Node.js, no 
  dependencies.'" (blog.cloudflare.com/kitesurf, Aug 6 2026).

  Caveats on what that actually means:
  - It was conceptual inspiration, not code reuse. Cloudflare's first attempt
    literally ported Obscura to Workers, and that attempt "didn't work very
    well" — they scrapped it.
  - The shipped Kitesurf architecture is built from Blitz (rendering), Stylo
    (Firefox's CSS parser), Boa (JS engine), and Parley (fonts) — none of which
    are Obscura's code. Obscura gave them the idea ("no-Chromium, agent-first
    browser"), not the implementation.

  So: real endorsement of the concept Obscura pioneered, from a credible source,
  but it doesn't validate Obscura's own rendering fidelity or
  production-readiness — Cloudflare needed a completely different, much larger
  engineering effort (Blitz+Stylo+Boa, 215k+ web-platform tests passing) to get
  an agent-browser that actually works. Doesn't change my earlier recommendation
  on Obscura being too risky for your YouTube transcript scraping specifically.

❯ is the cloudflare browser version available and free to use?                  

● Web Search("Cloudflare Kitesurf browser availability pricing free open source
            self-host")
  ⎿  Did 1 search in 6s

● Fetch(https://blog.cloudflare.com/kitesurf/)
  ⎿  Received 36.7KB (200 OK)

● Available, currently free — but not a fit for your setup. Kitesurf launched
  Aug 6 2026 inside Cloudflare's Browser Run product, free during beta with
  per-account limits, no post-beta pricing announced yet.

  The problem: it's not open-source and not self-hostable — it runs exclusively
  on Cloudflare Workers infrastructure (V8 isolates, Workers RPC), requires a
  Cloudflare account, and Cloudflare hasn't shipped the source yet (they've only
  said they plan to open-source it "hopefully soon," no date). That makes it an
  external paid-vendor platform with a temporary free tier — which conflicts
  with your hard rule of 100% free/self-hosted only, no SaaS-with-a-free-tier
  (the same reasoning that already excludes Jina/Cohere/OpenAI from your
  embedding picks). Even setting that rule aside, tying YCS's transcript
  pipeline to a beta product on someone else's cloud, with unknown future
  pricing and unproven YouTube/Shadow-DOM compatibility, is a worse bet than the
  self-hosted Patchright swap already recommended.

● One thing before editing: Obscura is bound to 127.0.0.1:9222 (host loopback
  only) — pods running inside your k3d cluster cannot reach that, only processes
  on the host itself can. For the pods to see it, Obscura needs to bind to all
  interfaces, and the code needs to target k3d's host-gateway DNS name instead
  of localhost.

  Recreate the container like this (kill/remove the current one first):
  docker rm -f obscura
  docker run -d --name obscura -p 9222:9222 h4ckf0r0day/obscura
  curl -s http://localhost:9222/json/version

  Now editing params.py:

● Update(apps/fastapi/domains/ycs/transcript/params.py)

● Confirmed connection to Obscura worked, but extraction fails immediately and
  completely:

  - CDP handshake succeeded: [cdp] Resolved: 
    ws://172.22.0.1:9222/devtools/browser..., 5/5 contexts warmed fine (~15ms) —
    so Obscura does accept Playwright's browser/context-creation calls.
  - 100% failure on the actual fetch: both videos failed identically after ~32s
    with Page.evaluate: TypeError: Cannot read properties of undefined (reading 
    'evaluate'). This error originates inside Playwright's own driver, not
    YouTube's page content — it means Playwright's internal Page/Frame wrapper
    never got a valid execution context to call .evaluate() against.
  - This lines up with the exact risk flagged in the earlier sota-search:
    Obscura claiming CDP "compatibility" at the protocol level (contexts/targets
    create fine) doesn't guarantee it emits the full event lifecycle
    Playwright's client library depends on internally (e.g.
    Runtime.executionContextCreated correctly bound to a frame). PATH 1/2 of
    your extraction cascade (_fetch_via_get_panel/_fetch_via_get_transcript) are
    both page.evaluate()-based in-page fetches — so this single incompatibility
    breaks the primary and secondary paths outright, not a selector/content
    issue.
  - Downstream behaved correctly: extract_videos didn't crash (per-video failure
    handling worked as designed, fetch_failed: 2), Qdrant/Neo4j both cleanly
    no-op'd on zero transcripts. The pipeline's error handling isn't the problem
    here.

  Net: this is a real, reproducible Playwright↔Obscura incompatibility at the
  driver level, not a fluke — same error, same timing, both videos.

❯ /sota-search the best way to use Playwright via MCP connected to CDP server   
to make the current process faster                                              

● Web Search("Playwright MCP server CDP connect_over_cdp performance 2026
            faster")
  ⎿  Did 1 search in 6s

● Web Search("@playwright/mcp changelog 2026 CDP endpoint remote browser")
  ⎿  Did 1 search in 6s

● Web Search("Playwright MCP vs direct Playwright automation speed overhead
            scraping pipeline")
  ⎿  Did 1 search in 7s

● Framing

  One-sentence restatement: does routing Playwright-over-CDP through an MCP 
  server make YCS's transcript-extraction pipeline faster, or is direct 
  Playwright the right call? Sub-questions: what does Playwright-MCP actually
  add on top of raw connect_over_cdp; is there a documented speed/throughput
  case for it; does it fit a deterministic, high-volume Celery pipeline vs. its
  actual design target.

  Recommendation

  Don't add MCP here — it's the wrong tool, and every source confirms it moves 
  you in the opposite direction. MCP servers (Microsoft's @playwright/mcp, or
  forks like mcp-playwright-cdp) exist so an LLM agent can drive a browser
  step-by-step via tool calls; every action requires a model round-trip, which
  is inherent, unavoidable overhead a deterministic pipeline doesn't have today.
  YCS's transcript service already calls Playwright directly over CDP with zero
  LLM involvement — that's already the fast path MCP would be trying (and
  failing) to approximate.

  How to apply it

  Nothing changes in your code. If the goal is genuinely "make the current
  process faster," the actual levers are all on the direct-Playwright side you
  already have: concurrency/pool tuning (MAX_CONCURRENT/CONTEXT_POOL_SIZE), the
  resource-blocking list, the get_panel/get_transcript in-page-fetch cascade
  (already bypasses DOM interaction — this is the real reason it's fast), and
  infra-level RAM/CPU headroom for the headed pod. None of that benefits from an
  MCP layer in front of it.

  Comparison table

  Approach: Direct Playwright + connect_over_cdp (current)
  Maturity: Stable, official Playwright API
  Latest (date): Playwright itself is continuously released
  Key trade-off: Full programmatic control, zero per-action overhead,
  deterministic
  When to pick: Any production/high-throughput pipeline — this is what you
  should
  keep
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Approach: @playwright/mcp (Microsoft, official)
  Maturity: Active
  Latest (date): v0.0.78 (Jul 14 2026)
  Key trade-off: Every step = LLM round-trip; benchmarked ~84% slower than
  Selenium when paired with an agent; huge token cost (114k vs 27k tokens/task
  vs CLI)
  When to pick: Only when an LLM agent needs to decide what to click/read next
  (e.g. exploratory QA, Claude Code driving a browser) — not for a fixed
  extraction routine
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Approach: mcp-playwright-cdp (community fork, Lars Hagen)
  Maturity: Stale
  Latest (date): last tag v0.2.10 (Feb 2025)
  Key trade-off: Same agentic-overhead problem, plus over a year without a
  release
  When to pick: Not recommended — unmaintained relative to the official package
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Approach: Cloudflare Browser Rendering's CDP+MCP endpoint
  Maturity: New
  Latest (date): Apr 10 2026
  Key trade-off: Exposes CDP to MCP clients on Cloudflare's hosted browser infra

  — a paid platform, and still agent-oriented
  When to pick: Excluded — paid SaaS, and still the wrong pattern for a
  deterministic pipeline

  Evidence

  - MCP adds a model round-trip per action, is not built for throughput: Speed &
    Scalability: Playwright MCP vs Selenium
  - Benchmark: Selenium ~83.76% faster than Playwright MCP + Claude; token cost
    114k (MCP) vs 27k (CLI) per task: Playwright CLI vs MCP — Better Stack,
    Testcollab: Playwright CLI
  - Explicit guidance that large-scale scraping needs dedicated infra, not
    step-by-step agent decisions: same Better Stack source.
  - @playwright/mcp v0.0.78, Jul 14 2026, supports cdpEndpoint/--cdp-endpoint to
    attach to an existing Chromium debug port: microsoft/playwright-mcp,
    Playwright MCP configuration docs
  - mcp-playwright-cdp fork last released Feb 2025 (stale):
    lars-hagen/mcp-playwright-cdp

  Caveats & unknowns

  - If there's a different reason you want MCP here (e.g., wiring an LLM agent
    to occasionally handle a novel YouTube UI change interactively, not the
    steady-state extraction path), that's a legitimate but entirely separate use
    case from "make the current process faster" — say so and I'll scope that
    separately.
  - No source benchmarks MCP specifically against YouTube; the ~84%-slower
    figure is a general Selenium-vs-MCP+Claude comparison, not this exact stack
    — but the mechanism (per-action LLM round-trip) is architecture-level, not
    target-specific, so it generalizes.

● That's a legitimate use case — MCP as a dev-time tool for me to inspect the 
  live page, not a production runtime change. Here's the setup, using the same
  headed CDP pod your pipeline already uses:

  1. Port-forward the real CDP endpoint to your host (it's ClusterIP-only, not
  reachable from outside the cluster):
  kubectl port-forward -n playwright svc/playwright-headed 9222:9222

  2. Register Playwright MCP in Claude Code, pointed at it:
  claude mcp add playwright-cdp -- npx @playwright/mcp@latest --cdp-endpoint
  http://localhost:9222
  (or drop this into .mcp.json at the repo root if you want it
  project-scoped/shared:)
  {
    "mcpServers": {
      "playwright-cdp": {
        "command": "npx",
        "args": ["@playwright/mcp@latest", "--cdp-endpoint",
  "http://localhost:9222"]
      }
    }
  }

  3. Workflow this actually enables: I navigate to a real YouTube watch page
  through the same browser your pipeline runs against, run ad-hoc
  page.evaluate()/network inspection live (inspect ytInitialPlayerResponse, the
  get_panel/get_transcript innertube response shapes, DOM structure if YouTube
  ships a UI experiment), diagnose exactly why a path is failing, then edit
  domain.py/service.py directly based on what I actually observed — instead of
  guessing from logs alone.

  Why this doesn't contradict the earlier answer: that recommendation was
  against putting MCP in the runtime pipeline (agent-per-request = slower). This
  is different — it's me, interactively, at your request, when something breaks
  or YouTube changes, not a per-video production path. Zero runtime/pod changes
  needed.

  Want me to run the port-forward and register this now?

❯ so this is the best way possible to make AI analyzes the page source code and

● Web Search("chrome-devtools-mcp vs playwright-mcp 2026 AI debugging network
            inspection best")
  ⎿  Did 1 search in 8s

● Web Search("best MCP server AI agent analyze page source adapt scraper script
            2026")
  ⎿  Did 1 search in 7s

● No — good catch to double-check. For automation (driving clicks/navigation),
  Playwright MCP is right. But your actual goal — "AI reads the page's
  network/source and figures out what changed" — is a debugging task, and
  there's a more precise tool for exactly that: Chrome DevTools MCP (Google's
  official server). It exposes full request/response bodies, console output, and
  performance traces directly — Playwright MCP's tools are action-oriented
  (click/type/navigate) with much thinner introspection. Since your extraction
  paths are literally reading innertube API response bodies
  (get_panel/get_transcript), that's squarely Chrome DevTools MCP's strength,
  not Playwright MCP's.

  Current expert consensus (multiple 2026 comparisons agree): run both — they're
  complementary, not competing. Same CDP connection pattern applies:

  # same port-forward as before
  kubectl port-forward -n playwright svc/playwright-headed 9222:9222

  # add alongside playwright-cdp
  claude mcp add chrome-devtools -- npx chrome-devtools-mcp@latest --browserUrl
  http://localhost:9222

  Practical split once both are registered: I'd use Chrome DevTools MCP first to
  see exactly what YouTube's page is sending/returning (network bodies, console
  errors, confirm whether get_panel changed shape or a new gate appeared) —
  that's the "analyze" half. Then Playwright MCP to reproduce the actual
  click/navigation sequence if the fix requires DOM interaction, not just an
  API-shape change — that's the "verify the adapted script still works" half.
  Then I edit domain.py/service.py based on both.

  Want me to register both now?

● Yes — same pattern as Playwright MCP, confirmed via the official docs
  (chrome-devtools-mcp supports --browserUrl to attach to any existing
  CDP-debuggable Chrome, which is exactly what your playwright-headed pod on
  COELHO Cloud is).

  1. Port-forward the same service (if not already running from before):
  kubectl port-forward -n playwright svc/playwright-headed 9222:9222

  2. Register it in Claude Code:
  claude mcp add chrome-devtools -- npx chrome-devtools-mcp@latest
  --browserUrl=http://127.0.0.1:9222
  or in .mcp.json:
  {
    "mcpServers": {
      "chrome-devtools": {
        "command": "npx",
        "args": ["chrome-devtools-mcp@latest",
  "--browserUrl=http://127.0.0.1:9222"]
      }
    }
  }

  That's it — no local Chrome install needed since --browserUrl skips launching
  one and attaches directly to the real headed Chromium your production pipeline
  already uses. Want me to run the port-forward and register it now?

✻ Cogitated for 35s

❯ what is the permanent install?                                                

● Two things needed for a true permanent setup — persistent registration + a
  real install instead of an on-demand npx fetch each time:

  1. Install the package globally (once):
  npm install -g chrome-devtools-mcp
  which chrome-devtools-mcp   # confirms the binary path

  2. Register it at user scope (persists across all projects/sessions, not just
  this repo — default claude mcp add scope is local, project-only):
  claude mcp add chrome-devtools --scope user -- chrome-devtools-mcp
  --browserUrl=http://127.0.0.1:9222

  This points Claude Code straight at the installed binary (no npx@latest
  version-resolution/network check on every launch) and the user scope means
  it's available in every project going forward without re-adding it.

  Same applies to Playwright MCP if you want it permanent too:
  npm install -g @playwright/mcp
  claude mcp add playwright-cdp --scope user -- playwright-mcp --cdp-endpoint
  http://127.0.0.1:9222

  One caveat either way: the --browserUrl/--cdp-endpoint still points at
  127.0.0.1:9222, which only exists while your kubectl port-forward is running —
  that part isn't "permanent," it needs to be re-run each dev session (or
  turned into a background/systemd-managed forward if you want that automated
  too).

  same headed CDP pod your production pipeline uses, so what I inspect through

  One thing to flag: this is your production Playwright pod — any interactive
  session I open through MCP shares the same browser/context pool the pipeline

  Worth checking Ingestion page/Flower for an in-flight run before I connect for

● No — correcting my earlier "run both" advice. I looked at Chrome DevTools
  MCP's actual tool list, and it's a full superset for this use case: it has
  both inspection (get_network_request, list_network_requests, evaluate_script,
  list_console_messages, take_screenshot) and full input-automation (click,
  fill, type_text, navigate_page, wait_for). It does everything Playwright MCP
  would do here.

  Playwright MCP's only real differentiator is cross-browser support
  (Firefox/WebKit) for CI test-suite promotion — irrelevant to you, since YCS
  only ever targets Chromium via CDP. The "run both" advice from those
  comparison articles is aimed at QA teams doing cross-browser testing, not a
  single-target debugging workflow.

  Just install Chrome DevTools MCP:
  npm install -g chrome-devtools-mcp
  claude mcp add chrome-devtools --scope user -- chrome-devtools-mcp
  --browserUrl=https://playwright-cdp.tail39dc94.ts.net

  That alone covers both halves — "see what the page is doing" and "reproduce
  the click/navigation sequence" — with one tool, one install.

● Findings from ~/COELHOCloud/infrastructure/modules/playwright/:

  - The "password" prompt is noVNC's web client asking for the VNC password
    (x11vnc -rfbauth, wrapped with -forever -shared) — VNC auth is inherently
    per-TCP-connection, there's no "session length" setting on the x11vnc/server
    side to extend.
  - I checked the whole deployment + ingress templates — there's no explicit 
    short idle-timeout configured anywhere (no nginx idle timeout, no Tailscale
    ingress timeout annotation) that I could just bump up. So "increase the
    time" isn't a config value that exists to tune; something (likely the
    browser tab backgrounding/throttling the WebSocket, or a normal Tailscale
    connection renegotiation) is dropping the connection, and noVNC's default
    behavior is to fall back to its connect screen — showing the password field
    again — on every drop.
  - Your second ask — only re-prompt on a genuinely new browser session, 
    silently reconnect otherwise — maps exactly to noVNC's own built-in feature
    for this: reconnect=true + reconnect_delay=<ms> URL params. It's the
    documented mechanism for this exact problem (community-reported as
    version-dependent in some edge cases, but it's the correct lever, not a
    workaround).

  The one place this URL lives is the Homepage tile annotation in
  ingress-novnc.yaml.tpl:25:
  gethomepage.dev/href: "https://${tailscale_hostname}.${tailscale_domain}/vnc.h
  tml?autoconnect=1&resize=scale"

  Proposed change — add &reconnect=true&reconnect_delay=3000:
  gethomepage.dev/href: "https://${tailscale_hostname}.${tailscale_domain}/vnc.h
  tml?autoconnect=1&resize=scale&reconnect=true&reconnect_delay=3000"

  This only fixes the Homepage tile link, though — if you bookmark/type the URL
  yourself elsewhere, you'd need the same query params appended there too. Want
  me to make this edit (and you terragrunt apply it yourself per your usual
  workflow), or do you access noVNC through a different URL I should target
  instead?

  (k8s-operator/api.md, the authoritative source) confirms ProxyClass has no 
  idle-timeout, keep-alive, or session-duration field at all. Its only fields
  are statefulSet, metrics, tailscale.acceptRoutes,
  useLetsEncryptStagingEnvironment, and staticEndpoints. I also checked the
  operator's values.yaml.tpl chart values and found nothing there either. A
  related GitHub issue (#14282) about operator connection timeouts turned out to
  be an unrelated UDP-blocked networking bug, not a tunable setting.

  Conclusion: there is no timeout value to increase in values.yaml.tpl or 
  anywhere in the Tailscale operator/ProxyClass layer — it doesn't expose one,
  so this isn't a config gap on your end. The connection drop is happening
  somewhere Tailscale doesn't let you tune (browser tab throttling, or normal
  WireGuard tunnel behavior), and VNC's protocol requires re-auth on every fresh
  connection regardless.

  That leaves the noVNC-level fix from before as the only real lever —
  reconnect=true&reconnect_delay=3000 on the vnc.html URL, which auto-retries
  the dropped connection in the background using the same page session's
  credentials, only re-prompting if you actually reload/reopen the page. Want me
  to make that edit to ingress-novnc.yaml.tpl now?

  ⎿  Did 1 search in 7s

● Web Search("Playwright overhead vs raw CDP websockets python performance
            benchmark 2026")
  ⎿  Did 1 search in 7s

● Framing

  One-sentence restatement: given a working CDP-based Playwright pipeline whose
  real bottleneck is waiting for YouTube's own JS to populate two page globals
  (not rendering, not our automation overhead), which specific 2026-current
  Playwright/CDP techniques actually cut wall-clock time, and which are
  marketing noise for this workload?

  Recommendation

  Switch wait_until="domcontentloaded" → wait_until="commit", keep everything 
  else architecturally as-is. That's the one change with a real, low-risk,
  evidence-backed payoff for this specific bottleneck; the other four candidate
  optimizations are either negligible or actively risky given what's already
  known about this codebase's YouTube-specific failure modes.

  Verdicts, in priority order

  Q1 — early-binding trap via addInitScript/CDP addScriptToEvaluateOnNewDocument
  instead of polling: NOT WORTH IT.
  page.wait_for_function polls via requestAnimationFrame inside the browser (not
  a Python-side round-trip loop) — it already resolves within ~1 frame (~16ms)
  of the site assigning ytcfg/ytInitialPlayerResponse. The real wait is
  YouTube's own script executing, which no earlier-binding trick shortens —
  you'd still be waiting for the same script to run, just with a getter instead
  of a poll. This trades real complexity (injecting a proxy/getter, handling the
  case where YouTube's own code does Object.defineProperty collision checks — a
  known bot-detection surface per the CDP-detection research) for zero
  measurable gain.

  Q2 — commit vs domcontentloaded: DO THIS.
  goto()'s wait_until only controls when Python regains control, not how long
  your subsequent wait_for_function polls run. Right now, Python sits blocked
  until DCL fires, then starts polling for ytInitialPlayerResponse/ytcfg — even
  though those polls don't need DCL, only the inline bootstrap <script> tags to
  have executed (which happens during parsing, before DCL). Switching to commit
  returns control to Python as soon as response headers arrive, and the existing
  polls start immediately instead of waiting out the rest of the parse. This
  removes real dead time with no behavior change, since your gates already
  wait_for_function on the actual data, not the DOM.

  Q3 — CDP Network.setBlockedURLs/Fetch.enable vs Playwright page.route(): NOT 
  WORTH IT.
  Evidence here is thin and inconsistent (marketing-style benchmark claims
  contradicting each other — "CDP recommended" vs "route() gives 40% boost" from
  different sources, neither well-sourced). Even if CDP-level blocking shaves a
  few ms per intercepted request, that's noise against the ~2.5s DCL-class wait
  that dominates your actual per-video budget. Not worth the engineering
  complexity of bypassing Playwright's route API.

  Q4 — pre-warming with an idle pre-navigated YouTube page: DO NOT DO THIS (real
  risk for this codebase specifically).
  General 2026 guidance favors context/page reuse over fresh browser launches —
  which you already do (5 pooled contexts). But "keep a blank youtube.com page
  navigated and idle between uses" is a different, riskier idea: your own code
  already has a documented workaround (_close_stale_cdp_targets) for YouTube's
  service workers accumulating on long-lived Chromium sessions and crashing
  connect_over_cdp's target-attach logic. Deliberately keeping idle YouTube
  pages alive longer would compound exactly that problem. Keep contexts reused
  (current behavior), but navigate fresh (about:blank → real URL) each time as
  you do now.

  Q5 — raw CDP/websockets instead of Playwright: NOT WORTH IT.
  Confirmed real: Puppeteer's native-CDP path is ~15-20% faster than Playwright
  on Chromium (11KB vs 326KB of WS traffic per action), because Playwright
  injects more JS for cross-browser compatibility you don't need here. But that
  overhead lives in the sub-second range; your bottleneck is multi-second
  JS-population wait time on YouTube's side. Rewriting the pipeline off
  Playwright to reclaim ~15-20% of a small fraction of total latency is a large
  rewrite for a marginal, likely-imperceptible win here.

  Comparison table

  Change: commit instead of domcontentloaded
  Maturity: Stable Playwright API, documented lifecycle
  Evidence quality: Solid, ≥2 sources agree on ordering
  Expected win: Real, removes measurable dead time
  Risk: Low — your gates already re-check data, not DOM
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Change: addInitScript getter-trap
  Maturity: Stable API, but wrong tool here
  Evidence quality: Solid on mechanism, no evidence of speed win
  Expected win: ~0
  Risk: Adds bot-detection surface (defineProperty traps are a known
  fingerprinting vector)
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Change: CDP-level request blocking
  Maturity: Mature but marginal here
  Evidence quality: Thin/contradictory
  Expected win: Negligible vs multi-second wait
  Risk: Extra complexity for no proven gain
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Change: Idle pre-warmed YouTube page
  Maturity: Common general advice, wrong for this target
  Evidence quality: N/A — reasoning from your own code's history
  Expected win: Unclear
  Risk: Real — conflicts with documented SW-accumulation bug
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Change: Raw CDP instead of Playwright
  Maturity: Well-established performance delta
  Evidence quality: Solid (15-20% figure, 2 sources)
  Expected win: Small % of a small fraction of total time
  Risk: High — full rewrite

  Evidence

  - commit fires at response-headers-parsed, before DOM parse; domcontentloaded
    waits for full parse: Why page.goto() is slowing down your Playwright tests 
    in 2026 — BrowserStack, Waituntil Option in Playwright — BrowserStack
  - addInitScript/addScriptToEvaluateOnNewDocument runs at document_start,
    before the page's own first script, but timing-window residue exists and is
    a documented detection vector: The CDP addScriptToEvaluateOnNewDocument trap
    — crawlex.net
  - Puppeteer (native CDP) 15-20% faster than Playwright on Chromium; 11KB vs
    326KB WS traffic per action, attributed to Playwright's cross-browser
    JS-injection architecture: CDP vs Playwright vs Puppeteer — Lightpanda
  - Context/page reuse is the standard 2026 scaling recommendation over fresh
    browser launches: multiple sources in the ZenRows/ScrapingBee/webscraping.ai
    search results.

  Caveats & unknowns

  - Q3's sources were low-quality marketing blogs with contradictory claims —
    flagging as genuinely thin evidence, not confidently resolved either way; my
    "not worth it" verdict rests on the dead-time math (multi-second wait
    dominates), not on a clean primary-source comparison.
  - None of these sources tested against YouTube specifically — all guidance is
    generic Playwright performance advice, applied here via reasoning about your
    actual bottleneck (JS-global population time), not YouTube-specific
    benchmarks.

  Ready to apply the one real win (commit navigation) and re-test live via the
  connected CDP MCP session — want me to proceed?

✻ Cooked for 27s

❯ well, very fast. What are all other improvements to apply on playwright       
  scripts?                                                                      

  Searched for 1 pattern (ctrl+o to expand)

● Beyond the two already shipped, four more candidates — two are clear, safe
  wins; one is a real dead-config finding worth a decision; one needs careful
  live testing before committing:

  A. Parallelize the two independent global-waits (real win, do this).
  _get_player_state (waits up to 8s for ytInitialPlayerResponse) and
  _wait_for_innertube_context (waits up to 3s for ytcfg) currently run
  sequentially — worst case ~11s combined, when they're checking two
  independent, unrelated globals. Running them concurrently via
  asyncio.create_task/gather cuts worst-case wait to max(8s, 3s) = 8s. The one
  thing to preserve: the current code short-circuits immediately if the video
  turns out to be unplayable (skips the innertube-context wait entirely) — I'd
  keep that by cancelling the innertube-context task if player-state comes back
  unplayable, so playable videos (the common case) get the parallelization win
  and unplayable videos keep the fast-exit.

  B. Merge two page.evaluate() calls into one (real win, do this).
  _get_player_state and _get_caption_tracks both read from
  window.ytInitialPlayerResponse but issue two separate page.evaluate()
  round-trips (each a full Python↔driver↔browser CDP hop). Merging the
  caption-track extraction into _get_player_state's existing evaluate call
  removes one full round-trip per video, for free — no behavior change, same
  data source.

  C. Dead config found — needs your call, not a silent fix.
  BROWSER_REFRESH_INTERVAL=15 and self._videos_since_refresh are tracked
  (incremented per video, reset on refresh) but nothing ever checks the counter 
  against the threshold — I grepped the whole file, there's no if 
  self._videos_since_refresh >= self.browser_refresh_interval anywhere. The only
  actual refresh trigger is reactive (_check_browser_health() failing
  is_connected()). So either: (1) this was meant to proactively recycle the
  browser every 15 videos to avoid CDP staleness and the wiring got dropped, or
  (2) it was deliberately left reactive-only after testing showed proactive
  refresh wasn't needed, and this is just leftover config. Want me to wire it
  up, or leave it and treat it as dead config to delete?

  D. Raise MAX_CONCURRENT/CONTEXT_POOL_SIZE above 5 — plausible, but needs a 
  live-monitored trial, not a blind edit.
  The 5-context cap exists because "5 concurrent pages OOMKilled at 2Gi" — but
  that ceiling was measured before today's blocklist expansion (stylesheets,
  fonts, peripheral player JS all now blocked), so each page's real memory
  footprint should be lower now. There may be headroom for 6-7 concurrent
  contexts on the pod's 4Gi limit, which would raise batch throughput directly.
  This one I don't want to just ship — I'd want to bump it by one, watch the
  pod's actual memory via kubectl top during a real batch, and back off if it
  creeps toward the limit.

  Want me to apply A + B now (both are pure wins, no trade-offs), and separately
  decide on C and D?

✻ Churned for 1m 3s

❯ this pool size is used via asyncio.semaphore?                                 

● No — they're two separate mechanisms, currently just set to the same number so
  they don't conflict:

  - asyncio.Semaphore(max_concurrent) gates how many _fetch_single_attempt
    coroutines can be running at once — it wraps the entire per-video attempt
    (async with self.semaphore: at the top of _fetch_single_attempt), before a
    context is even acquired.
  - self._context_pool is a separate asyncio.Queue(maxsize=context_pool_size)
    holding actual pre-warmed browser-context objects. _acquire_context() pulls
    from it (falls back to creating a temporary extra context if starved for
    30s), _release_context() returns it.

  context_pool_size defaults to max_concurrent (both are 5 today via
  MAX_CONCURRENT/CONTEXT_POOL_SIZE) specifically so the two never fight each
  other — every task that gets past the semaphore is guaranteed to find a
  context waiting, per the code's own comment ("Pool size should match
  max_concurrent to avoid context-creation storms").

  This matters for the concurrency-bump idea (point D): raising MAX_CONCURRENT
  alone without also raising CONTEXT_POOL_SIZE would create artificial
  contention — tasks would pass the semaphore then queue up waiting on
  _acquire_context(), eventually spawning uncontrolled temporary contexts past
  the warm pool once the 30s fallback kicks in. Both constants need to move
  together, exactly as the current 5/5 pairing already assumes.

✻ Sautéed for 30s

❯ about A and B changes, it is too risky to trigger captchas and make we lose   
  this big win?                                                                 

● No — A and B carry essentially zero CAPTCHA risk. Here's why, concretely:

  - A (parallelize the two waits) only changes the order in which Python's own 
    code polls already-loaded browser state — wait_for_function/evaluate are
    local, read-only checks of window.ytcfg/window.ytInitialPlayerResponse. They
    don't send any network request, don't touch the DOM, don't click anything.
    YouTube's servers have no visibility into "did the client check these two
    things sequentially or concurrently" — there's nothing for a server-side or
    even client-side bot-detector to observe here.
  - B (merge two evaluate() calls into one) reduces the count of CDP
    Runtime.evaluate commands — again, a debugging-protocol-level operation
    between Playwright and the browser, invisible to the page's own JS and to
    YouTube's servers. It doesn't change what network requests fire or when.

  Neither touches the actual bot-detection surface: request volume, request
  timing/rate against YouTube's servers, navigation patterns, or CDP protocol
  leaks (the Runtime.enable class of signal from the earlier Patchright
  research). They're purely internal scheduling/efficiency changes on data
  that's already sitting in the page.

  The one change on the table that's actually worth being cautious about is D 
  (raising concurrency past 5) — more simultaneous requests from one IP is the
  kind of thing that plausibly correlates with triggering rate-based bot
  detection, which is exactly why I flagged it as needing a live-monitored trial
  rather than a blind ship. A and B don't carry that risk profile at all — safe
  to apply independent of any captcha concern.

✻ Cooked for 27s

❯ what are all best fixes to ship in a row?                                     

  Searched for 1 pattern, read 1 file (ctrl+o to expand)
  ⎿  Interrupted · What should Claude do instead?

❯ what are all best improvements to ship in a row?                              

● Four items, in this order:

  1. A — Parallelize the two independent global-waits (_get_player_state +
     _wait_for_innertube_context via asyncio.gather/create_task, preserving the
     unplayable-video short-circuit by cancelling the innertube wait if
     player-state comes back unplayable). Zero risk, real win (~cuts worst-case
     wait from ~11s to ~8s).
  2. B — Merge _get_caption_tracks into _get_player_state's single evaluate() 
     call. Both read the same ytInitialPlayerResponse object via two separate
     CDP round-trips today; merge into one. Zero risk, removes one round-trip
     per video.
  3. C — Resolve the dead BROWSER_REFRESH_INTERVAL config. It's tracked
     (_videos_since_refresh increments every video) but never actually checked
     against the threshold anywhere — only reactive health-check-triggered
     refresh exists today, and that's been working fine in every run we've
     observed. My recommendation: delete the dead tracking rather than wire up a
     proactive refresh, since wiring it up would only add teardown/rewarm
     overhead (~1-2s per 15 videos, per the observed "Initializing with CDP" +
     "Warmed context" cost) with no demonstrated reliability gain. Your call if
     you'd rather wire it up instead.
  4. D — Raise MAX_CONCURRENT/CONTEXT_POOL_SIZE from 5 → 6-7. Real potential
     throughput win now that per-page memory footprint is lower (today's
     blocklist expansion), but this is the one that needs a live-monitored trial
     (kubectl top during a real batch) rather than a blind ship — the 5-cap
     exists because of a real OOM measured before today's changes. Ship this
     last, separately, with monitoring ready.

  Want me to proceed with A + B now, and apply my C recommendation (delete the
  dead config), then handle D as its own careful step after?

  Searched for 7 patterns, read 3 files (ctrl+o to expand)

● Neo4j timeout question — verified in code, concrete answer

  No, Neo4j does not use the same tuned client. I traced the actual call path:

  - DD's hot-path nodes (doc_distill/off_topic/chapter_assign) go through
    _get_async_openai() in domains/llm/rotator/chain/service.py — a shared, 
    pooled AsyncOpenAI client: HTTP/2, 200/100 connection pool, a
    deliberately-tuned 90s timeout ceiling, and max_retries=0 with an explicit
    comment: "rotator handles cascade, SDK retries would triple timeout."
  - YCS's Neo4j chain (build_ycs_neo4j_pinned_chain →
    build_reduce_label_chain(), same file, line 642) builds a bare
    ChatOpenAI(base_url=..., api_key=..., model=..., temperature=0.0) — no
    timeout, no max_retries override, no connection pooling, a fresh instance
    per call (not even cached).

  That means Neo4j's LLM calls fall back to the openai-python SDK's own defaults
  — a 600s timeout and max_retries=2 — completely bypassing the tuning that
  exists two functions away in the same file. This is exactly the failure mode
  the "would triple timeout" comment warns about, just currently unapplied here:
  a slow call can get SDK-level-retried (with its own backoff) before the
  rotator's own arm-swap/circuit-breaker logic ever sees a clean failure to
  react to. That's a very plausible real contributor to Neo4j being "predictable
  but slow" — not the rotator itself being slow, but this call site silently
  stacking an extra, redundant retry layer on top of it.

  Should it use the shared config? Yes — same reasoning that justified tuning
  _get_async_openai() applies identically here. This is a small, targeted fix
  (route build_reduce_label_chain() through the pooled client, or at minimum
  pass timeout=90.0, max_retries=0 explicitly), not a rewrite.

  Should this move into COELHO LLM Rotator itself? Partially already true, and
  the rest shouldn't move. The rotator already owns the
  provider-cascade/arm-swap logic server-side — that part is shared infra,
  benefiting every consumer automatically. What can't sensibly live server-side
  is the client's own outer-bound timeout/retry-count, since different consumers
  have genuinely different budgets (a Celery task with a 3660s hard time_limit
  vs. a synchronous FastAPI request path needing to fail fast). That's
  inherently a per-consumer setting — which is exactly what
  _build_timeout/_get_async_openai() already is: shared, Nexus-side, sitting
  under domains/llm/rotator/, not under domains/dd/. Nothing needs transferring
  between repos; Neo4j just needs to actually use the shared client that already
  exists two functions away instead of bypassing it.

  Phase-split UI — feasible, with one real caveat

  Checked the actual progress-reporting code: the current single "Phase 1 ·
  Playwright & ElasticSearch" bar's tick signal already comes purely from
  Playwright resolving each video (fetch_batch's on_video_done fires the instant
  Playwright succeeds/fails, before that chunk is even sent to ES).
  ES-transcript-indexing currently has zero progress signal at all — it happens
  silently, per-chunk, riding invisibly at the end of each Playwright chunk.

  So: splitting is real work, but asymmetric —
  - Phase 1 (Playwright): free — just relabel/re-key the existing bar, the
    granular per-video signal already exists.
  - Phase 2 (ElasticSearch): needs one new progress-callback hookup around
    index_transcriptions_to_elasticsearch() in fetch_transcriptions_batch
    (small, well-scoped, not a rewrite) — but it will tick in chunk-sized jumps
    (0→10→20→24), not smooth per-video, since ES gets bulk-written per chunk.
    Worth deciding upfront whether a "batchy" bar next to Playwright's smooth
    one reads as intentional or as broken in the UI.

  Renumbering to 1-4 (Playwright/ES/Qdrant/Neo4j) is a pure labeling change on
  top of whichever of the above you pick.

  Want me to implement the fix for the Neo4j timeout gap first (it's the
  smaller, more clearly net-positive change), or start with the Phase-split UI?

❯ why we should transfer this from Docs Distiller to COELHO LLM Rotator?        

● Fair pushback — my earlier answer only addressed the within-Nexus bug (fix the
  one call site), but your original question was about future, non-Nexus
  consumers too, and that's where "transfer" actually earns its keep. Here's the
  sharper version:

  The proof this matters: it just failed inside the same codebase. The correct
  pattern (max_retries=0, 90s timeout, pooled client) already exists 40 lines
  away from Neo4j's broken call site, in the same file, and still got missed. If
  a correctly-configured client is that easy to bypass by accident within one
  repo where the author can literally see the fix nearby, a brand-new project
  talking to the rotator from scratch has ~zero chance of discovering "set
  max_retries=0, timeout ≥ 90s" on its own — there's nothing about the rotator's
  API surface today that tells a client this is required.

  What can actually move, and what can't:
  - Can't move: the client's own outer-bound timeout number — that's inherently
    per-consumer (a Celery task with a 3660s budget vs. a synchronous request
    path needs different values), so it can never be fully server-enforced.
  - Can move: the contract/knowledge of how to configure a client well — right
    now it only exists as unwritten tribal knowledge inside
    domains/llm/rotator/chain/service.py's comments. That should live with the
    rotator itself: either (a) a documented SLA — "the rotator's own cascade
    completes or gives up within N seconds, so any client should set
    max_retries=0 and timeout ≥ N" — published in the rotator's own docs, or (b)
    a thin official client-config recipe/package any project can pull in,
    instead of every new consumer re-deriving it from scratch (or missing it,
    like Neo4j just did).

  Recommendation: don't move code out of Nexus (there's nothing DD-specific to
  relocate — the client already lives at the shared Nexus-app level). Instead,
  add a short "how to configure a client against this rotator" section to
  ~/Workbench/COELHOLLMRotator/docs/rotator-roadmap.md (or a new
  client-integration doc) documenting the exact max_retries=0 + timeout-floor
  pattern and why — so it's discoverable by any future project, Nexus or not,
  without needing to read Nexus's source first.

  Want me to draft that doc section, and separately fix Neo4j's actual call site
  to match it?

❯ take a look at how doc distiller graph node from Planner on Docs Distiller    
  works, it is, analyze its codes. Is it better to transfer its timeout and     
  requests engine to within COELHO LLM Rotator instead? It is the same request  
  engine remains untouched, but the rotation with timeouts engine goes to llm   
  rotator, does it make sense?                                                  

● Now that I've actually read chat_judge_bandit_async (the real "engine" DD's
  Planner nodes call), the picture is clearer than my last answer — and your
  framing needs one correction: the rotation part has already moved server-side.
  The timeout part can't follow it, for concrete reasons visible right in this
  function.

  Evidence from the code:
  - chat_judge_bandit_async takes dd_process and candidate_filter params — both
    explicitly commented # ignored — no per-process weights and # ignored — no 
    heavyweight filter. That's the fossil record of a client-side bandit that
    used to pick between candidate deployments/weight them per-process, now
    stripped down to no-ops because the rotator absorbed that job. There is no
    loop over candidate models here anymore — it's one
    client.chat.completions.create(...) call to COELHO_ROTATOR_MODEL (a generic
    identifier), full stop. The rotator decides which real provider/model
    actually serves it.
  - Nexus's only remaining input to that decision is extra_body: {"metadata": 
    {"rotator_task": f"dd-{dd_process}"}} — a namespace hint so the rotator's
    own bandit can keep DD's learning separate from other callers'. That's the
    correct shape: client says what kind of work this is, server decides how to 
    route it.

  What's still client-side, and why it can't move:
  1. timeout_s — the per-call SDK timeout. This is fundamentally "how long is
     this specific caller willing to wait," and that varies by consumer: a
     Celery task with a 3660s hard limit vs. a synchronous judge call needing a
     fast answer. No single server-side value serves every consumer's actual
     patience — this has to stay a client decision.
  2. backstop_s = timeout_s + 15, an outer asyncio.wait_for — the code's own
     comment explains why: httpx's read-timeout measures gaps between chunks,
     not total call duration, so a connection that trickles data or goes
     silently stale (NAT/LB drop) can outlive the timeout without ever raising.
     That's a blind spot in this client's HTTP library, invisible to the server
     — the rotator has no way to know or fix that its response is dribbling out
     too slowly for httpx to notice.
  3. max_retries=0 on the pooled client — and the comment is explicit that the
     backstop's correctness depends on this: "Safe to rely on here because
     max_retries=0 above means there's no internal SDK retry loop that could eat
     this budget out from under it." This is a local SDK config flag with no
     wire representation — there's no field in an OpenAI-compatible request that
     says "my client's local retry count is 0." The rotator literally cannot
     observe or enforce this over the protocol; it can only be a client-side
     convention.

  So the honest answer: your framing ("request engine stays, rotation+timeout
  engine moves") doesn't match what's actually there. It's already split
  correctly — rotation/arm-selection is server-side (rotator), and what remains
  client-side isn't leftover "rotation" logic, it's three things that are
  inherently about this specific client's patience budget and this specific 
  client's HTTP library quirks, none of which the server can decide on the
  client's behalf. The real, still-open bug is just that Neo4j's call site
  doesn't reuse this same client/pattern at all — that's the actual fix, not an
  architecture move.

❯ so it should keep into DD's COELHO Nexus, right? And we should only apply it  
  adjusted to YCS, right?                                                       
● Yes, exactly right on both counts.

  Keep it in Nexus — there's no DD-specific code to relocate; the pattern
  (pooled client, max_retries=0, tuned timeout + backstop) already lives at the
  shared Nexus-app level (domains/llm/rotator/chain/service.py), not inside
  domains/dd/. Nothing needs moving to the rotator repo — the rotator already
  owns the part that's actually its job (arm/provider selection).

  Apply it to YCS, adjusted — the adjustment is real and specific:
  chat_judge_bandit_async talks to the rotator via a raw AsyncOpenAI client
  (_get_async_openai()), but YCS's Neo4j path uses LangChain's
  LLMGraphTransformer.aconvert_to_graph_documents(), which requires a LangChain 
  ChatOpenAI object, not a raw client — so build_reduce_label_chain() can't just
  switch to the raw pattern directly. The fix is to configure ChatOpenAI itself
  with the equivalent settings, since langchain_openai.ChatOpenAI accepts the
  same underlying knobs:

  ChatOpenAI(
      base_url=COELHO_ROTATOR_URL,
      api_key=COELHO_API_KEY,
      model=COELHO_ROTATOR_MODEL,
      temperature=0.0,
      timeout=GRAPH_BATCH_TIMEOUT_S,   # or a dedicated per-call constant, not 
  the 600s batch watchdog
      max_retries=0,                    # rotator handles cascade — same 
  reasoning as chat_judge_bandit_async
  )

  Want me to implement this now — updating build_reduce_label_chain() (or
  splitting a Neo4j-specific variant so DD's own callers of that function aren't
  affected) with these settings?

❯ is this necessary to build an unified engine to serve both DD and YCS at the  
  same time in the same way? What are all best next steps to ship in a row?     

  Searched for 1 pattern (ctrl+o to expand)

● Not strictly necessary to unblock today's fix — but a small, cheap unification
  is worth doing, because the same gap already exists twice. I checked:
  _get_chat_llm() (DD's own "legacy LangChain wrapper... retained for
  non-hot-path callers") has zero real callers today — but it also never sets
  max_retries=0, the exact same gap as YCS's build_reduce_label_chain(). It's
  dormant now, but it's proof this pattern drifts the moment there are two
  independent ChatOpenAI(...) construction sites instead of one. Consolidating
  both into a single shared builder costs almost nothing and removes that drift
  risk permanently — for both today's live bug and tomorrow's dormant one.

  What should not unify: the raw-AsyncOpenAI hot path
  (chat_judge_bandit_async/_get_async_openai) stays separate from the LangChain
  path — they exist for different consumers (max-performance structured judge
  calls vs. LangChain-ecosystem interop like LLMGraphTransformer), and merging
  them would add LangChain overhead to calls that specifically avoid it.

  Best next steps, in order:

  1. Add one shared _build_chat_openai(*, timeout_s, **kwargs) -> ChatOpenAI 
     helper in chain/service.py — encapsulates timeout=timeout_s, max_retries=0
     (mirroring _build_timeout's role for the raw-client path).
  2. Refactor _get_chat_llm() to use it — fixes the dormant bug, zero risk (no
     live callers).
  3. Give YCS's Neo4j path its own timeout constant and route through the same 
     helper — not DD's 90s default. Transcripts run up to ~20K chars, meaning
     bigger prompts/completions than DD's typical judge calls; the real observed
     504s from the rotator resolve in ~20-30s, so I'd set this at 120s
     (headroom above real failure latency, well under the existing 600s
     GRAPH_BATCH_TIMEOUT_S outer watchdog) rather than copying DD's 90s blindly.
  4. Compile-check + redeploy + verify pod sync (same discipline as today's
     earlier changes).
  5. Live-test on a real Neo4j run — reuse a batch with known-flaky videos (e.g.
     the ones that previously hit 504s) and compare against the
     earlier-observed baseline (504 → SDK retry → arm-swap chain) to confirm
     max_retries=0 actually cuts the compounding delay.
  6. Optional: append this fix + evidence to today's docs file once validated.

  Want me to start with steps 1-3 now?

● Clean. Now redeploying and verifying pod sync, then let's answer the speed

  Ran 1 shell command (ctrl+o to expand)

❯ skaffold dev not running, go ahead                                            

● The code change is in place; it just needs skaffold dev running to redeploy —
  go ahead and start it whenever you're ready to test.

  On "will it be faster": yes, for a specific reason — it removes
  double-retrying, not raw request latency. Concretely:

  - Before: build_reduce_label_chain()'s bare ChatOpenAI(...) had no max_retries
    override, so it fell back to the openai SDK's own default (max_retries=2).
    When the rotator returned a 504 (observed taking ~20-30s in practice), the
    SDK itself would silently retry 1-2 more times with its own backoff before
    neo4j_task's own circuit-breaker/arm-swap logic ever saw a clean failure to
    react to. That's compounding delay hidden a layer below where the app's own
    retry/swap logic operates.
  - After: max_retries=0 means the first 504 surfaces immediately to
    neo4j_task's own logic — which already has real, working circuit-breaker +
    arm-swap handling (we watched it work correctly earlier this session: 3
    consecutive failures → swap arm → residual requeue).

  What this fixes: wasted time from SDK-level retries stacking underneath the
  app's own retry logic.
  What this does NOT fix: the rotator's own upstream 504s still happen — that's
  a real, separate latency source (whatever's timing out server-side),
  unaffected by this change. So expect fewer wasted seconds per failure, not
  elimination of failures themselves.

  Also set an explicit 120s per-call timeout (vs. the previous implicit 600s SDK
  default) — sized for transcripts' larger prompts, comfortably under the
  existing 600s outer watchdog — so a genuinely-stuck call now fails and
  triggers the circuit breaker sooner too, instead of silently consuming most of
  the 600s watchdog budget on one bad call.

  Once skaffold's up, best test: rerun a batch that historically hit real 504s
  (Raiam Santos videos CN69kSHKPHM/QqjMnMDOeeo triggered them earlier) and
  compare failure-to-swap timing against the earlier logs.

✻ Brewed for 14s

❯ so in this case, will Neo4j graph knowledge building connected to AI provider 
  work in the same way as doc distiller graph node from Planner does?           

● Aligned on the config discipline that mattered (the actual bug), but not an
  identical path — worth being precise about which parts now match and which
  don't:

  Now the same:
  - max_retries=0 — SDK-level double-retry eliminated on both paths.
  - No client-side arm/model picking — both send model="auto"-equivalent and let
    the rotator's own bandit decide server-side.
  - An explicit, deliberate timeout instead of an implicit SDK default (90s for
    DD's hot path, 120s for Neo4j — different values because DD's judge calls
    are small, Neo4j's transcripts are large, but same principle: pick a real
    number, don't let a 600s default hide a stuck call).

  Still genuinely different, by design:
  - Interface: DD's hot path (chat_judge_bandit_async) uses a raw AsyncOpenAI
    client for minimum overhead. Neo4j goes through LangChain's ChatOpenAI
    because LLMGraphTransformer.aconvert_to_graph_documents() requires a
    LangChain BaseChatModel object — it can't take a raw client. This was
    already established as correct in our earlier discussion: don't force these
    into one code path, they serve different interface requirements.
  - Connection pooling: DD's hot path shares one pooled AsyncOpenAI client
    (HTTP/2, 200/100 connections) across every call via _get_async_openai()'s
    singleton. Neo4j's ChatOpenAI builds its own separate httpx client under the
    hood — not sharing that pool. This is a real remaining asymmetry, not fixed
    today.

  Could full parity go further? Yes — langchain_openai.ChatOpenAI accepts
  http_async_client, so _build_chat_openai() could be handed the same pooled
  client _get_async_openai() uses, closing that last gap. I didn't do that yet
  since it wasn't part of the diagnosed bug (the double-retry was the actual
  problem); happy to add it if you want full parity, but it's a smaller,
  lower-urgency win than what's already shipped.

✻ Cogitated for 20s

❯ how many parallel requests doc distiller Planner's graph node does with these 
  settings?                                                                     

  Searched for 1 pattern (ctrl+o to expand)

● 10 parallel calls — doc_distill (the Planner node using
  chat_judge_bandit_async) gates concurrency with asyncio.Semaphore(CONCURRENCY)
  where CONCURRENCY = 10, plus a 70.0s per-call TIMEOUT_S (its own explicit
  override, not the generic 90s ceiling — different from the ceiling I mentioned
  earlier).

  That 10 isn't arbitrary — the comments show real tuning history: 24 →
  saturated free-tier rate limits (402s/timeouts), 16 → still burst-limited, 10
  is the current settled value that avoids hitting per-minute quota windows
  while staying meaningfully parallel. The underlying pooled client
  (_build_limits()) allows up to 200 total connections/100 keepalive, so 10
  concurrent calls has huge headroom in the connection pool itself — the real
  ceiling is the upstream provider's rate limit, not anything on the Nexus side.

  Worth disambiguating the terminology since your question uses "graph node" for
  both: DD's doc_distill is a LangGraph node (part of the Planner's execution
  graph) — a different "graph" from YCS's Neo4j knowledge graph being built by
  neo4j_task. Neo4j's own concurrency is separate: EXTRACT_CONCURRENCY = 3 (from
  graph_builder/params.py, overridable via YCS_NEO4J_CONCURRENCY) — much lower
  than doc_distill's 10, tuned for that pod's own constraints, not this
  client-config discipline we just fixed.
✻ Brewed for 24s

❯ can we run these same 10 concurrent requests for Neo4j YCS process?           

● Technically yes — it's one constant (EXTRACT_CONCURRENCY in
  graph_builder/params.py, or the YCS_NEO4J_CONCURRENCY env override) — but I'd
  caution against blindly matching 10. Different workload shape, and DD's own
  history argues against it.

  Why 10 doesn't transfer directly:
  - doc_distill's calls are small: 600 max_tokens, summarizing into ~60 words +
    key terms, p99=47.6s. Neo4j's calls are large: full transcripts up to ~20K
    chars in, hundreds of entities/relationships as structured output out (we
    watched one video produce 470 nodes/647 rels). Same concurrency level burns
    far more tokens/sec against the same shared free-tier providers behind the
    rotator.
  - DD's 10 is itself a downward correction, not a target to copy: their own
    comments show 24 → rate-limit saturation (402/timeouts) → 16 → still
    burst-limited → 10 settled. That's evidence that more concurrency made 
    things worse for a lightweight workload — a strong signal that a heavier
    workload (Neo4j) would hit that ceiling at an even lower concurrency, not
    the same one.
  - We already have direct evidence of strain at concurrency=3: the real 504
    Gateway Timeouts and arm-swaps we watched fire earlier this session happened
    at today's current EXTRACT_CONCURRENCY=3. Jumping straight to 10 risks
    amplifying that failure rate rather than improving throughput — more
    parallel heavy calls hitting an already-strained free-tier quota.

  Recommendation: if you want more Neo4j throughput, test incrementally (e.g.
  3→4 or 3→5) with the same live-monitoring discipline as the Playwright
  concurrency trial — watch for an increase in 504s/arm-swaps as the signal to
  stop, not just wall-clock time. Don't reach for 10 as a first move; it's very
  plausibly past the cliff for this heavier workload, not under it.

  Want me to bump it to 4 or 5 and set up a monitored test run, or leave
  concurrency alone for now and consider this closed?

  Searched for 2 patterns, read 2 files (ctrl+o to expand)

● This is the real root cause: only 10/24 videos ever got a genuine extraction
  attempt to completion — the other 14 (including 11 that were never attempted 
  at all, not failed) got silently abandoned when the circuit breaker tripped
  mid-segment. Traced it in graph_builder/service.py:293-362:
  asyncio.as_completed(pool) processes videos in completion order, and the
  moment 3 consecutive ones fail, it breaks out — any of the pool's other tasks
  that hadn't finished yet (started or not) are simply dropped from that
  segment's accounting. They do get retried next segment (via the real "already
  in Neo4j" DB-state check, not the misleadingly-named "residual" list), but
  with only MAX_ARM_SWAPS=3 (4 total segments) and each segment's breaker
  tripping after just ~5-6 attempts, the run ran out of segments before ever
  attempting 11 of the 24 videos even once.

  This bug is pre-existing, not something I introduced today — but today's two
  changes made it manifest far more severely: raising concurrency (3→5)
  increased real contention, and tightening the timeout (implicit 600s →
  explicit 120s) made triple-consecutive-timeouts much easier to trigger, so the
  circuit breaker tripped faster and more often per segment, burning through
  the whole arm-swap budget with a much smaller fraction of the pool ever
  attempted.

  Reverting concurrency now — the direct trigger for the increased trip
  frequency:

❯ does it make sense?                                                           

● Reasonably, yes — with one honest caveat worth naming.

  What's solid: the evidence genuinely clears concurrency of blame — identical
  failure (simultaneous 120s timeouts, 0 successes) reproduced at both
  concurrency=3 and concurrency=5, so there's no reason left to hold concurrency
  back. Re-enabling 5 is justified by data, not just "trying again."

  What's a real inference, not a measurement: 400s isn't derived from an actual
  percentile pull on Neo4j's own call latency — it's reasoning by analogy
  ("~600s implicit default was working before, so land somewhere under the 600s
  outer watchdog but well above 120s"). Compare that to DD's doc_distill, where
  the 70s timeout came from pulling real LangFuse p99 data (genuine successful
  calls topped out at 47.6s, max 51.7s) — a measured target, not a guess. I
  don't have that same telemetry for Neo4j's graph-extraction calls, so 400s is
  my best-reasoned estimate, not a verified ceiling.

  Net: worth testing, but if this run also shows timeout clustering (even at a
  longer interval), that's real data telling us the actual latency distribution
  — at that point pulling LangFuse traces for this specific call path (if it's
  instrumented) would replace guessing with a measured number, the same way DD
  did for doc_distill. Worth checking whether that instrumentation already
  exists before the next test, so a second miss doesn't mean a third blind
  guess.

✻ Baked for 11s

❯ is there a retry settings for neo4j on YCS? Just check it                     

  Searched for 2 patterns, read 1 file (ctrl+o to expand)

● Checked thoroughly — there's exactly one retry mechanism, and it's
  coarse-grained:

  - Celery task level: none. @app.task(bind=True, name=...) — no max_retries,
    autoretry_for, or retry_backoff. If the task itself raises unhandled, it
    just fails.
  - Per-video, within a segment: none. _extract_one() makes exactly one attempt
    per video per segment — a single transient hiccup (timeout, 429, whatever)
    immediately counts as a strike against the circuit breaker. No immediate
    re-try, no backoff.
  - HTTP/SDK level: explicitly disabled as of today's fix (max_retries=0) —
    deliberate, since the SDK auto-retrying would double up with the layer
    below.
  - Segment/arm-swap level: this is the only real retry that exists. A video
    that fails (or gets abandoned by a circuit-breaker trip) becomes part of the
    next segment's pool and gets a fresh attempt on a different model/arm —
    capped at MAX_ARM_SWAPS=3 (4 total attempts across the whole run, shared
    across all videos, not per-video).

  Worth contrasting with the Playwright side of YCS, which has two retry layers
  Neo4j doesn't: fetch_single()'s per-video retry loop with exponential backoff,
  and fetch_batch()'s whole-batch retry-pass loop. Neo4j has neither — a single
  bad response for one video costs it its only shot within that segment, and it
  only gets another chance if the arm-swap counter hasn't run out and it
  happens to land in the next segment's pool.

  This is directly relevant to what we just debugged: with no per-video retry,
  every transient failure goes straight to the circuit breaker, which is exactly
  why a 120s-too-tight timeout (or genuine provider hiccups) escalate to "burn
  the whole arm-swap budget" so fast.

  identified earlier — the circuit breaker abandons whatever's still queued in
  a segment the moment it trips, and only MAX_ARM_SWAPS=3 (4 total segments)

  of 4 segments) to burn the entire swap budget while 13 videos never got a
  clean shot. Total run time: 1026s (~17 min), most of it spent riding out
  repeated 504 sequences.

  This is the real remaining bottleneck, and no amount of concurrency/timeout
  tuning fixes it — it needs either raising MAX_ARM_SWAPS, or fixing the
  abandonment behavior itself (e.g., track exactly which videos were never
  attempted and prioritize them first on the next segment, instead of relying on
  the "already in Neo4j" skip-check to implicitly carry them forward). Want me
  to tackle that next?

      358 + * task, so a single poll's `meta.phase` string has to be inte
          +rpreted                                                       
      359 + * relative to EACH bar's own place in SPLIT_PHASE_ORDER — a p
          +hase that's

          +ached reads                                                   
      361 + * 0%, and the bar's own active phase reads its real current/t
          +otal. */                                                      
      362 +function _subPhasePct(prefix, state, meta) {                  
      363 +    if (state === "SUCCESS") return 100;                      
      364 +    if (state === "FAILURE" || state === "ERROR") return 100; 
      365 +    const m = meta || {};                                     
      366 +    const targetIdx = SPLIT_PHASE_ORDER.indexOf(SPLIT_PHASE_TA
          +RGET[prefix]);                                                
      367 +    const currentIdx = m.phase ? SPLIT_PHASE_ORDER.indexOf(m.p
          +hase) : -1;                                                   
      368 +    if (currentIdx < 0) return 0;                             
      369 +    if (currentIdx > targetIdx) return 100;                   
      370 +    if (currentIdx < targetIdx) return 0;                     
      371 +    if (m.total && m.current != null) {                       
      372 +        return Math.max(2, Math.min(100, (m.current / m.total)
          + * 100));                                                     
      373 +    }                                                         
      374 +    return 8;                                                 
      375 +}                                                             
      376 +                                                              
      377 +function _subPhaseLabel(prefix, state, meta) {

          +ed";                                                          
      380 +    if (state === "PENDING") return "Queued";                 
      381 +    const m = meta || {};                                     
      382 +    const targetIdx = SPLIT_PHASE_ORDER.indexOf(SPLIT_PHASE_TA
          +RGET[prefix]);                                                
      383 +    const currentIdx = m.phase ? SPLIT_PHASE_ORDER.indexOf(m.p
          +hase) : -1;                                                   
      384 +    if (currentIdx < 0) return "Queued";                      
      385 +    if (currentIdx > targetIdx) return "Done";                
      386 +    if (currentIdx < targetIdx) return "Queued";              
      387 +    if (m.current != null && m.total) return `${m.current}/${m
          +.total}`;                                                     
      388 +    return "Running";                                         
      389 +}                                                             
      390 +
